  command runs: before the plugin-state-ready gate that is the previous boot's `active`, and it stays
  so for the whole boot when this boot did not rewrite the file (an unreadable previous state, or a
  failed write, `index.ts`).
- **Where a plugin posts** (#243). `HostApi.announce(message)` keeps its signature (#219 added an optional named `destination`, below); it is `postForPlugin(<the plugin's name>, message, postDeps)`, with `postDeps` built once in `activate()`. It reads `routing.json` each time (no cache), asks `announceTargets` for the plugin's channels — every `postTo` it has, de-duplicated, in server-id order; none means `ANNOUNCE_CHANNEL_ID` — and posts to each in that order. A channel whose webhook is in `routing.json`, is not marked `broken` and has a URL in `routing.secrets.json` is posted through it; any other channel, and any webhook that failed, is posted to as the bot (`announceTo`, its `[announce]` line unchanged, and the webhook path prints the same one). A 401 or 404 marks the webhook `broken` (with the reason) and it is skipped from then on until it is added again; a 429, a 5xx, a timeout or an over-long message falls back to the bot for that post and marks nothing. With no routing, or none for the plugin, that is one post to `ANNOUNCE_CHANNEL_ID` — through that channel's webhook if it has a usable one, and as the bot otherwise, which is all it ever was before. Core's own release announcements do not go through this. **Named destinations (#219):** a plugin's manifest may declare `destinations: [{ name, description }]`; `announce(message, name)` then goes to every channel the operator mapped that name to (`servers[guild].destinations[name]` in `routing.json`, picked per server in the panel), through the same webhook-or-bot path. A name mapped in no server posts exactly where `announce(message)` would, and a name the manifest does not declare is logged once (`createHostApi`) and treated the same way. `discovery.json` lists each plugin's declared destinations for the panel, and a `routing-set` may map only those (`validatePluginRouting`'s `declared`, from `declaredDestinations`) — so a plugin not yet loaded cannot have destinations mapped until it has been.
- **The channel gate** (#243). A plugin's chat-input command is put to `gateCommand` by `handleCommand` (`index.ts` hands it the plugin that owns the command, `whereOf` as a thunk, `readRouting` and `readDiscovery`); core commands never reach it, and buttons and modals are not gated, since they belong to a message that is already in an allowed channel. When the plugin's routing for that server lists channels, the command runs in them and in a thread whose parent is listed; anywhere else the reply is ephemeral and names the channels (`<#id>` is a channel mention, so Discord shows the name and links it). No entry for the server, `"all"`, a DM and a plugin with no entry all allow. The channel's own id decides first, and `whereOf` — which fetches the channel from Discord when it is not cached — is called only when that id is not listed, so a plugin with no routing, a listed channel, a DM and a core command cost no fetch (a DM or a core command does not even read routing). The gate fails open, and says so: it logs a `[gate]` line when it lets through a command it could not judge (the channel could not be looked at, so its parent is unknown) and when the gate itself fails (`readRouting` rejects; for a file it cannot parse it does not, it reads it as fresh, which allows everything, and logs that in its own `[routing]` line). It is advisory wherever the bot cannot look (#482). A refusal names only listed channels that `discovery.json` still shows in the server (the file is refreshed every 15 minutes and not at all when a refresh fails, so the staleness cuts both ways, and a listed channel the bot cannot view is not in it and counts as gone; a server listed with no channels, an outage stub, is not read as "all gone": a channel deleted since can be named once, and one hand-added to `routing.json` that is not yet in the file counts as gone, which lifts the restriction if it was the only one listed); routing is not pruned on a channel delete.
- **Acting on an update** (#104): `/plugins update <name>` writes a transient `targetVersion` and
  restarts into it now; `/plugins update <name> at:<HH:MM UTC | ISO-8601-with-offset>` stores a
  `scheduled` entry that the `pluginUpdates` check runs when due — looked at on every 60 s tick
  (`runDuePluginSchedules`), not only the 15-minute index poll (#442) — heads-up DM, then a restart; a
  schedule whose version is already installed, or whose version or time is unreadable, is dropped inside the
  arbiter and the Discord requester it saw told by DM (#441).
  `remind`/`skip`/`cancel` set `remindAt` / `skippedVersion` / clear the pending update; a `remind` also
  records the version as notified (so the next tick does not notify it and wipe the snooze) and lifts a skip of
  that version, never stepping an already-notified newer version back (#501). On the boot
  after an update the bot DMs the requester the outcome (✅ now on X / reverted-still-on-previous /
  reverted-and-not-running-either / installed-but-failed-to-start, or turned off), clearing
  `pendingReport` before delivery. A broken target reverts to
  the previous version (its cached bundle) — never a silent upgrade. Guards: the write subcommands are refused until
  `markPluginStateReady` so they can't race the boot write; a due schedule defers while a restart is
  already pending, and its serialized mutate is the sole arbiter so a racing `cancel` (which clears
  both `scheduled` and `targetVersion`) can't leave a queued update behind.

## Gotchas

- **`post`/`dm`/`edit` (#736) send as the bot, never through a channel's webhook, and `edit` only
  edits a message this bot authored.** A link button is a component, which a plain webhook cannot
  carry, and an edit needs a message the bot itself sent — `announce` is the one path still allowed
  to use a channel's webhook (ADR-0006 decision 5); it does not change. Content is validated before
  any Discord call: a bare `http(s)://` run in it is wrapped in `<...>` so it does not unfurl, because
  `MessageFlags.SuppressEmbeds` would also hide the card, not just suppress the link preview.
  Interactive `buttons` (#323) must carry a customId prefixed with the *calling* plugin's name —
  another plugin's prefix, or core's `report:`, is refused before any Discord call, so a message can
  never carry a button whose press would be routed somewhere other than its sender. Presses are not
  routing-gated (they belong to a message already delivered), and the plugin must still reply or
  defer itself within Discord's 3 s, exactly like any other component interaction. The prefix rule
  covers Host API messages only: an in-process plugin replying to a raw interaction can still attach
  any components it likes. `buttons` is also refused when the sending plugin declares no
  `interactions` (checked at call time through `HostDeliveryDeps.handlesInteractions`, since the
  HostApi exists before the module does). A press no running plugin claims -- plugin gone, disabled,
  not yet running, or without `interactions` -- is logged by prefix only and answered with an
  ephemeral "This button is no longer available." (`answerUnclaimedPress`), not Discord's "This
  interaction failed". **`edit` is bot-scoped, not plugin-scoped**: any plugin can edit any delivery
  this bot made, including another plugin's buttons, and an edit that gives only non-empty `links`
  (`{ links: [{ label, url }] }`) replaces every row, dropping the interactive buttons. An older
  host refuses `buttons` with "interactive buttons are not supported yet" -- the marker for a pre-#323
  host; a plugin can simply retry without `buttons` on any refusal.

- **`BOT_DATA_DIR` is a test seam and a production footgun (#139).** It relocates the whole data
  directory, which is what stops `bun test` reading and writing the developer's real
  `data/state.json` — the suite used to destroy `attemptedUpdateToSha` and `pendingUpdateReport` on
  every run, and `update.test.ts` had an `afterEach` that wrote the real file *on purpose* to
  mitigate it. In a deployment it is dangerous: `buildCreateSpec` copies the container's env onto
  the replacement but derives `Binds` from the mounts, so a value pointing off the named volume
  means the original and the replacement write **different filesystems** — the replacement's
  `handoff.json` is never seen, every `/update` waits out `HANDOFF_DEADLINE_MS` and reports a
  replacement that "never reported in", and self-update is broken with nothing naming the cause.
  It breaks the ops surface too: `bot-ops.sh` hardcodes `/app/data/...` (the `*_PATH` constants, `status`'s realm read and `plugin-request`'s mailbox dir), so `status`'s
  realm and plugin reads and `plugin-request` delivery would all be looking at the wrong directory.
  Mitigated by keeping it out of `.env.example` and `bot-ops.sh`'s `ALLOWED`, and by `src/bootLog.ts`
  logging the resolved path on every boot, synchronously, before `./config` can throw — that log is
  what turns the symptom into a one-line diagnosis — and `bootLog.test.ts` runs the module in a
  subprocess to pin it (#458). (Absolute-only is a *separate* guard against a relative
  value resolving against cwd back into a checkout; it does **not** help here, since the dangerous
  values are absolute.) The `bunfig.toml` preload is what sets it for tests, and it **must** be a
  preload: Bun runs every test file in one process with one module registry, so per-file
  `process.env` priming is first-importer-wins and a single new unprimed file sorting first would
  silently revert the whole protection. Note the preload is rooted at the repo — `bunfig.toml` is
  not walked up to, so `ops/admin`'s own package does not get it. Harmless today (no `ops/admin`
  test imports `src/`; `ops/bot-ops.test.ts` imports only `src/config` and `src/routing/model`, which
  read no data dir), and the bun-test refusal (`NODE_ENV=test`, or a `*.test.*` entry file,
  `src/dataDir.ts`) makes it fail loudly rather than corrupt if one
  ever does — but **run the suite from the repo root**. **#252:** the preload's cleanup of a
  previous run's `rackbops-bot-test-data-*` directory is bounded by LIVENESS AND AGE (a directory
  whose embedded pid — the name is now `<prefix><pid>-<random>` — is still alive and under an hour
  old is left alone), not "at most one directory" as it used to claim: sweeping every same-prefixed
  directory unconditionally deleted a CONCURRENTLY running suite's own `BOT_DATA_DIR` out from under
  it (two agent sessions' `bun test` runs on one machine is routine, not a corner case). See
  `test/sweep.ts` for the pure decision logic and its own tests. The sweep removes real directories
  only (`lstat`; a stray file or a planted link under the prefix is left alone), logs what it removed,
  and a removal that fails (a dead run's dir an orphan still holds as its cwd, on Windows) is warned
  and skipped rather than failing the run (#461); `test/setup.test.ts` runs the preload for real in a
  child `bun test` against directories planted in a private temp root (#403).
- **A test that leaves module-level state behind fails, in any order (the test-state guard).**
  The guard covers the module-level state of eleven modules, and for the same one-process reason as
  above every test file shares one copy of each: `restart.ts` (critical-section depth, a pending
  restart, an active handoff, a begun shutdown, `awaitCriticalIdle` waiters, `onStopRequested`
  listeners and whether they were notified, an armed restart-deferral deadline, registered `onBeforeExit` hooks), `config.ts` (the shared `config` object, against what
  was resolved), `storage.ts` (the corrupt paths a keep-mode read remembered, #477), `state.ts` (the shared `state` object, against what was loaded, and its writer's
  queue), `update.ts` (`checkInFlight`, the once-per-sha warning's latch and the last failed swap, #506), `announce.ts` (the tick guard; when discovery, releases,
  self-update and plugin updates were last polled; `pluginStateReady`; the repos recorded
  unreachable), `routing/live.ts` (the init context, the home-server warnings said, the
  registration queue), `routing/store.ts` (the `routing.json` warnings said, the before-repair copies made, the routing and secrets
  write queues), `plugins/host.ts` (the plugin `state.json` write queue, the `disposing` flag and the activation run in flight, #541), `plugins/requests.ts`
  (files remembered as undeletable, the drain queue) and `plugins/updates.ts` (failed-delivery
  counts). #385 was the cost: `restart.test.ts` tests ended mid-handoff, and whichever ran last
  made every later file's `checkForUpdate` answer `busy`, visible only in a randomized order.
  `bunfig.toml`'s second preload, `test/stateGuardHook.ts`, registers an `afterEach` that runs
  after every test in every file — after that file's own `afterEach` hooks, measured on Bun 1.4.2
  — and fails the test if any module's `…StateForTest()` snapshot differs from what its reset
  hooks leave, naming the module and each leaked field (a queue counts only while it still has
  work pending). It then waits, up to `QUEUE_DRAIN_MS` (1 s), for the pending work on every queue a
  snapshot holds (the four write queues, the registration queue, the request drain) — the fresh
  queues the reset hooks start would not stop a job already running — and runs every reset hook,
  queues included, so one leak fails one test rather than every test after it. If the wait runs out
  the message says so, and the stuck queue is dropped all the same (a write still running then can
  overlap a later one on the same file). Two kinds of job can still
  change guarded state during the next test and fail that one too: one on those queues that
  outlasts the wait, and one on no queue at all — an update check, a release poll, a tick — which
  is not waited for, and runs on after its flag or poll time is reset, possibly against `config` or
  `state` already put back under it. It has to be a second preload: `announce.ts` and the routing modules read
  the environment `test/setup.ts` primes when they load, and a static import in `setup.ts` would be
  hoisted above that priming. A side effect: `config.ts` now loads for every run, even of one file
  that never imports it; `test/setup.ts` forces `DISCORD_TOKEN` and `ANNOUNCE_CHANNEL_ID` and clears
  the sixteen other keys `config.ts`, `logFormat.ts` and `plugins/install.ts` read at import (#462;
  `NODE_ENV`, `HOSTNAME`, `BOT_ENV_FILE` and the handoff keys are left alone and change no result), so
  a shell's values for those never reach the suite or the subprocesses it spawns, and cannot
  stop a run. A leak made
  inside a test fails that test, whatever the order — unless that test's own `afterEach` throws,
  since Bun then skips the guard for it. Such a leak, and one made outside a test — by a
  `beforeAll` or `afterAll`, or by async work that finishes after its test — is charged to
  whichever test runs next, possibly in the next file, and goes uncaught if no test runs after it
  (nothing is left to inherit it then). Left outside the check, on purpose: `restart.ts`'s exit
  stub (`setExitFn` hands back its own restore, and `restart.test.ts` leaves its stub installed
  after its last test, which only ever swallows a stray exit), `announce.ts`'s `tickGeneration` (a
  tick only compares it with the one it took), `routing/live.ts`'s `lastRegistrations` (unread
  without a context, cleared by `initRouting`), the temp-file counters in `storage.ts`,
  `plugins/index.ts` and `ops/admin/server.ts` (they only keep temp names unique), `bootLog.ts`'s
  `dataDir` (set once at load), `index.ts`'s globals (no test imports it — `index.test.ts` reads its
  source), constant lookup tables (`REPORT_PROJECTS`, `LIMITS`, `CLIENT_OPTIONS`, ... — exported
  objects nothing writes to, in code or tests), and process-wide globals that are not module state
  at all (`globalThis.fetch`, `process.env`, `console`), which tests stub and restore themselves.
  Guarded state must not carry from one test to the next, so clean up by the end of each test — an `afterEach`
  calling the module's reset hook, as `restart.test.ts`, `routing/live.test.ts` and
  `routing/store.test.ts` do; `config` and `state` changes are put back by hand (or with
  `resetConfigForTest()` / `resetBotStateForTest()`), and a write or job a test queues is awaited
  before it ends — not dropped with `resetRoutingWritesForTest()`, `resetPluginHostForTest()`,
  `resetStateWriterForTest()`, `resetRoutingQueueForTest()` or `resetPluginRequestDrainForTest()`,
  which are the guard's own and would hide a leaked write or job from it. The resets tests do call
  leave every queue alone, so a job a test leaks still reaches the guard after that test's own
  `afterEach`. Each such reset of a module that holds a queue — `resetBotStateForTest()`,
  `resetRoutingForTest()`, `resetRoutingWarningsForTest()` and `resetPluginRequestsForTest()` —
  has a test in its module's own test file pinning that, one per queue it leaves alone
  (`plugins/host.ts`'s `resetPluginHostForTest()` is the guard's own; the `disposing` flag it also clears (#541) has a flag-only `resetPluginDisposingForTest()` that a test calls in its `afterEach` after `disposePlugins`, and that leaves the write queue and the activation run to the guard, pinned in `plugins/host.test.ts`). (`resetRoutingForTest()`
  and `resetPluginRequestsForTest()` used to replace their queue too, so cleanup that called them
  inside a test — `routing/live.test.ts`'s and `plugins/requests.test.ts`'s `afterEach`,
  `announce.test.ts`'s `finally` blocks — dropped a leaked job before the guard looked.) The
  table is `test/stateGuard.ts`, the decisions `test/stateLeaks.ts`;
  `test/stateGuard.test.ts` runs `test/stateLeak.fixture.ts` — which leaks from the table's first
  and last modules, leaves a request drain running, and leaves a write that never finishes — as a
  child `bun test` to prove the real hook reaches both ends of the table, waits for the running job,
  stops waiting for the stuck one and says so, and resets after each leak (the `.fixture.ts` name
  keeps discovery from running it in the main
  suite); that every row is checked and reset, and that every row holding a queue has a reset, is
  pinned by the table-identity, queue-reset and fake-table loop
  tests in the same file.
- **`admins.json` is written through one serialised mutator per process, with a per-call temp name
  (#228).** The panel's `AdminStore.mutateDynamic` (`ops/admin/server.ts`) is the `src/storage.ts`
  `writeJsonAtomic`/`createJsonWriter` parallel for the admin allow-list: two concurrent
  `POST`/`DELETE /api/admins` calls used to each read the same `admins.json`, decide independently,
  and write the WHOLE list back — the loser's add/remove silently dropped — and the real write used
  one FIXED temp path (`${adminsFile}.tmp`) for every writer, so one call's rename could land on a
  file another was still writing, corrupting the file. `serializeAdminMutations` queues every
  mutation on a promise chain (read fresh → hand the decision a COPY → write only if it returns
  `next`), so the read-modify-write the admin-removal floor check and the POST no-op check depend on
  runs against a set no sibling call can still change out from under it — the same reason
  `handleAdmins`'s own `list()` reads through `mutateDynamic` too (a no-op turn) rather than a bare
  `readDynamic()`, so a response never races ahead of a sibling mutation still queued ahead of it.
  `writeAdminsFile`'s temp name carries this process's pid and a per-call counter
  (`${adminsFile}.${pid}.${counter}.tmp`) — no cross-process token the way `src/storage.ts`'s
  `TMP_TOKEN` needs, since the admin panel has no multi-container handoff the way the bot does. The
  one path that still reads `readDynamic()` directly, unqueued, is `handleRequest`'s per-request
  auth check — every request needs it, and queueing it behind admin-list writes would stall
  unrelated requests for no benefit.
- **The admin asset proxy is bytes end to end (#229).** `AdminAssetResult.body` (and
  `makeAdminAssetFetcher`'s fetch) never decodes — the pre-#229 `new TextDecoder().decode(buf)` step
  UTF-8-decoded every fetched asset, so a binary one (a PNG, a font, a `.wasm`, gzipped JSON) came
  back with every non-UTF-8 byte turned into U+FFFD, silently, behind a 200 and the right content
  type. Latent until a plugin ships a non-text admin-tab asset (the only current use, a realm list,
  is JSON and survived by accident). `serveAdminBundle`/`servePluginProxy` hand the bytes to
  `Response` unchanged.
- **An env key read from the Plugin Index has one shape, checked on both sides of the bridge
  (#226).** `ENV_KEY_RE` (`ops/admin/server.ts`, `/^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]*$/`) filters `mergePluginsView`'s
  `envKeys` and `env` — a manifest entry declaring a key with a newline could otherwise smuggle a
  second `env-set` line past a plugin's own-keys scope (`bot-ops.sh` reads stdin one line at a
  time), since nothing upstream of the merge validates a key's shape. `index.html`'s
  `PLUGIN_ADMIN_HELPERS` block carries an equal literal (the page can't import a server module) for
  `buildSetEnvBody`'s own refusal — pinned equal to the server's by a source-pin test, since a drift
  between the two would let one side accept a key the other refuses. This is a defence-in-depth
  narrowing on top of ADR-0005 decision 6's accepted posture (an admin bundle already has the
  panel's own authority) — see #221's ADR-0005, and issue #226 itself for why the isolation posture
  is accepted rather than sandboxed.
- **A promise that never settles does not fail a Bun test on Windows: it hangs the run (#259).** With
  nothing else pending, Bun 1.3.14 on Windows neither applies the per-test timeout nor exits — a control
  with a live timer beside it fails cleanly at 5 s, the lone never-settling promise does not — so a
  deadlock (the bug the test is there to catch) turns into a stuck process rather than a red test, and a
  mutation run that reintroduces it hangs the runner. Any test that could deadlock races its await against
  a deadline (`withinDeadline` in `live.test.ts`) so it fails by name, and a scripted run of the suite
  passes `--timeout` and its own spawn timeout as well.
- **Plugin ticks are `running`-gated, and `activatePlugins()` runs BEFORE `startScheduler()`.**
  `activate()` in `index.ts` awaits `activatePlugins()` — which flips each `LoadedPlugin.running`
  true once that plugin's `activate()` has succeeded (and `disposePlugins` has not begun, #541) — and only *then* calls `startScheduler(client,
  pluginTicks(loaded))`. So the scheduler's first (synchronous) tick already sees
  `running:true` and fires each plugin's ticks at boot, preserving the boot-time announcements the
  core WoW checks used to make on that first tick (the wow plugin owns them now, #107). `pluginTicks`
  still wraps each tick to run only while `running` is true, kept as defence in depth (a tick that
  somehow fired before activate is skipped, not crashed). Don't reorder these so the scheduler starts
  first — that reintroduces the boot-announcement gap this ordering closes; `index.test.ts` pins it.
  The same `running` flag gates every other dispatch into a plugin — HTTP routes, slash commands
  (#407), component, modal and autocomplete handlers — and `disposePlugins()` clears it before calling
  any `dispose()` (#409), so a plugin that has not finished activating, failed to, or is being disposed is
  handed nothing new. Where the dispatch awaits something first (the routing gate, a request body), the
  flag is read again after it. A new dispatch path into plugin code needs the same check (declaration
  reads like `build()` before activation, and `activate()`/`dispose()` themselves, are not dispatches).
  A shutdown that lands during activation is handled twice (#541): `disposePlugins` waits, bounded, for the activation in flight, and `activatePlugins` disposes the plugin whose `activate()` was pending once it settles and refuses the ones after it, while `activate()` in `index.ts` stops after the loop (`isShuttingDown()`) instead of writing state or reporting for a boot that never happened. Because everything after it waits on `activatePlugins()`, it is bounded per plugin (#408): an
  `activate()` still unsettled after `PLUGIN_ACTIVATE_TIMEOUT_MS` (30 s), like a bundle `import()` after
  `PLUGIN_IMPORT_TIMEOUT_MS` (10 s) in `loadPlugins()`, is recorded as that plugin's failure, so a hung
  plugin delays the scheduler, the HTTP listener, routing and the boot state write by the bound, not forever.
  Work a plugin starts in the background (a timer, a listener, an un-awaited promise) is outside all of
  this: Bun 1.4 exits on an uncaught throw or an unhandled rejection, and the host installs no
  process-level handler, so such a throw ends the whole bot (#470; the contract says so on `activate()`).
- **Plugin extraction shells `tar`, which is present in `oven/bun:1-slim`** (verified — the
  Acceptance runs `docker run --rm oven/bun:1-slim tar --version`). `src/plugins/install.ts`'s
  `tarExtract` runs `tar -xzf … --strip-components=1 package/dist/plugin.js package/package.json`.
  The real-`tar` unit test is `skipIf(win32)` because Windows `tar` reads a `C:\` drive-letter path
  as a remote host; the container is Linux, so production is unaffected.
- **A plugin's bundled `discord.js` must resolve to the host's single `/app/node_modules` copy**, or
  `instanceof`/builder identity breaks. Bundles are built `--external discord.js`, so the `import`
  stays bare and resolves by walking up from `data/plugins/<name>/<version>/dist/` to `/app/node_modules`
  — there must be exactly one copy on the running instance. (D1; the review Acceptance pastes
  `import.meta.resolve("discord.js")` from inside the container.) It holds only while the data dir is
  under `/app` (the default `/app/data`): a `BOT_DATA_DIR` outside it leaves every bundle's bare
  `discord.js` import unresolvable. And nothing at install or load time checks it — a bundle built
  without `--external discord.js` loads its own copy, and the breakage shows only when an
  `instanceof` or builder check runs (#497).
- **Fork gotchas.** This repo was extracted, with history, from `nazumods/wow`'s
  `apps/warbandeer-discord` — see the top-of-file note for full credit to Nazuraki.
  `GITHUB_REPO` now defaults to this fork (`Rackbops/rackbops-discord-bot`), and self-update no
  longer path-filters (`BOT_PATH` is gone from `src/update.ts` / `src/redeploy.ts` — the whole
  repo is the bot now, so `fetchLatestBotSha` compares against the newest commit on the branch,
  full stop, and `buildRemote` builds from the repo root with no subdirectory). Remaining open
  work from the fork extraction:
  - ~~Self-update's rebuild needs `GITHUB_REPO` to be publicly clonable~~ — **resolved**: this
    repo is public (`Rackbops/rackbops-discord-bot`), so the daemon's remote git build context
    (no credential support) already works, for both self-update and the no-clone deploy model
    below.
  - `ops/bot-ops.sh`'s consumers — `apps/warbandeer-desktop`'s and `wow-companion`'s **Ops**
    tabs, plus the shared `apps/bot-ops` backend — live in the original monorepo and
    `roshne/wow-companion`, not here. The script itself still works over a direct SSH
    invocation (see `ops/README.md`'s "Run directly on the box" example). A caller must also
    pass `BOT_OPS_CONFIG_DIR` + `BOT_OPS_COMPOSE_FILE` (see the config-dir gotcha below).
    ~~Neither app's `ops.json` has a field for them~~: **resolved** by
    [roshne/wow-companion#197](https://github.com/roshne/wow-companion/issues/197). The shared
    backend gained `configDir`/`composeFile`/`scriptPath` target fields (nazumods/wow#940), set
    together or not at all. See `ops/README.md`'s "Enabling a panel" example.
  - ~~No CI~~ — **resolved**: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` now runs `bun run check` + `bun test`
    on every pull request (`on: pull_request` — not a direct push to `main`), a fork-native
    workflow rather than a carry-over of the original monorepo's path-scoped
    `discord-bot-test.yml`.
- **Config dir and compose file are two independent, required locations — never derived from
  each other or from a checkout.** `/opt/rackbops-discord-bot/<instance>/` holds `.env` +
  `backups/` (operator-precious, never auto-migrated — see `ops/README.md`); `.env` is never
  edited by anything but `ops/bot-ops.sh env-set` or the operator by hand. The **compose file**
  lives separately, at `/opt/stacks/rackbops-discord-bot-<instance>/docker-compose.yml`, purely
  so Dockge (nucbox's stack manager) lists and manages it — Dockge only recognises a compose
  file at a path under its own `DOCKGE_STACKS_DIR`, and doesn't care where anything that file
  references lives. `ops/bot-ops.sh` takes both as required env vars
  (`BOT_OPS_CONFIG_DIR`/`BOT_OPS_COMPOSE_FILE`), along with `BOT_OPS_PROJECT`/`BOT_OPS_CONTAINER`
  (issue #41 — a monorepo-era default on these last two once let a var-less invocation silently
  target a project/container no real deploy produces), with no fallback to its own script location
  or to any built-in name. **Both paths must be absolute; a relative one is rejected before docker
  is touched** (issue #60) — a relative path resolves against the invoking cwd, so hand-running the
  script from a checkout would point `env-set` at the *checkout's* `.env` and write
  `backups/.env.bak.*` (a live token) beside it, next to the `admins.json` `.gitignore` doesn't
  cover. Those backups are never pruned automatically, by design (#474: the config dir is operator
  data; `ops/README.md` gives the manual prune). `src/dataDir.ts`'s `resolveDataDir` cites this as the absolute-only precedent for
  `BOT_DATA_DIR`. `ops/install.sh` bootstraps a fresh instance's layout; see `ops/README.md` for
  the full runbook.
- **`docker-compose.yml`'s interpolation vars have TWO sources, in Compose's own precedence
  order: shell-exported values first, then a `.env` file in the stack directory** (Compose loads
  one automatically for `${VAR}` substitution — a mechanism entirely separate from the `env_file:`
  directive, which loads secrets *into* the container). `ops/install.sh` writes that second source
  itself: `/opt/stacks/rackbops-discord-bot-<instance>/.env`, holding this instance's real
  `BOT_ENV_FILE`/`BOT_OPS_CONTAINER`/`BOT_OPS_PROJECT`/`BOT_OPS_CONFIG_DIR`/`BOT_OPS_COMPOSE_FILE`/
  `BOT_BUILD_CONTEXT`/`GIT_SHA` — generated and refreshed on every re-run exactly like the compose
  file itself, never hand-edited. Its whole purpose is Dockge: Dockge's own Start/Stop/Restart
  buttons run `docker compose` with **none** of `install.sh`'s printed shell prefix, so before this
  file existed those buttons (and any bare `docker compose` typed by hand) fell straight through to
  `docker-compose.yml`'s own `:-` fallbacks — `env_file` pointing at a `.env` that was never there,
  `container_name` defaulting to the monorepo-era `warbandeer-discord` (issue #41). Shell-exported
  values still win when present, so `ops/bot-ops.sh`'s own explicit exports (`cmd_restart`,
  `cmd_env_set`) are unchanged — those still set `BOT_ENV_FILE`/`BOT_OPS_CONTAINER` per-invocation.
  `ops/install.sh`'s OWN printed step-2 command, by contrast, stopped doing this in #169: it used
  to shell-export `GIT_SHA`/`BOT_ENV_FILE`/`BOT_BUILD_CONTEXT`/`BOT_OPS_CONTAINER` too, redundantly
  with what the stack `.env` this bullet describes already carries — dropped once `install.sh`
  started writing that file itself, so the printed command now relies on the same auto-load
  Dockge's buttons always have (proven for an absolute `-f` path + an unrelated cwd, not just
  Dockge's cwd-equals-stack-dir case, in `ops/docker-compose.test.ts`). `docker-compose.yml`'s own
  `:-.`/`:-warbandeer-discord`/`:-.env` fallbacks are left in place on
  purpose — a plain `docker compose up -d --build` run directly from this checkout for local dev
  (documented in the compose file's own header) has no stack-directory `.env` to fall back to, and
  relies on exactly those defaults. Self-update's own rebuilds (`src/redeploy.ts`) were already
  remote-context-based before any of this, via the Docker Engine API directly, not `docker
  compose` — untouched by it.
- **Config lives in TWO files by design, a recorded deviation from the personal `CLAUDE.md`'s
  one-answer-file rule** (issue #169): the generated stack `.env` above (params) and the
  hand-edited `$CONFIG_DIR/.env` (secrets) are never merged into one — see `ops/README.md`'s
  "Why two files, not one answer file" and the repo's own `CLAUDE.md` for the reasoning.
- **`env-set`, `restart` and `recreate` hold one `flock` on the config dir (#227).** Two panel
  tabs (or two admins) saving at once used to both read the pre-change `.env` and the second `mv`
  silently discarded the first's keys — no error, both requests answered `{ok: true, ...}` — and
  two concurrent `up -d --force-recreate` on one compose project is separately undefined. The lock
  is `flock` on a file descriptor opened **read-only on `$CONFIG_DIR` itself**
  (`exec 9<"$CONFIG_DIR"`), never a lock file: a lock file written by `env-set` running as root
  inside the admin container would be unwritable by the deploy user's own SSH runs, while a
  directory needs only read+execute to open, which both identities have. Taken once, at the top of
  each of the three mutating subcommands (right after `guard_no_handoff_in_progress`), and held
  until the process exits — spanning the read, the diff, the backup, the rewrite and the recreate,
  which is exactly the span that must be exclusive. **Never taken inside `recreate_bot()`**: `flock`
  is per *open file description*, so a second descriptor opened there while `cmd_env_set` already
  holds one would deadlock against the caller's own lock. A bounded wait (`flock -w
  "$LOCK_WAIT_SECONDS"`, `BOT_OPS_LOCK_WAIT_SECONDS`, default 60s — under the admin panel's own 90s
  `SUBPROCESS_TIMEOUT_MS`, so a save queued behind a normal ~20s recreate lands rather than 504s),
  then a refusal that has written nothing — one of `env-set`'s own `die "env-set: ..."` refusals,
  so #272's `failureWroteNothing` keeps the user's edits, the right outcome for "try again." `need
  flock` guards a host without it (util-linux, Essential on Debian, so present in `oven/bun:1-slim`
  and on the deploy host) into failing loudly rather than running unserialised. **On this Windows
  dev box Git Bash has no `flock` at all**, so `ops/bot-ops.test.ts`'s fixture provides a no-op
  shim on the fake PATH (only when a real one is absent) purely so the rest of that suite stays
  green here — the lock's actual concurrency behaviour is exercised only on CI's Linux
  (`REAL_FLOCK`-gated tests).
- **A profile-gated service still gets fully interpolated and validated at `docker compose
  config` time, regardless of which `--profile` is active.** Profiles only filter which services
  actually get *created/started*; the whole file is parsed and every service's variables
  resolved up front. Verified live: an earlier draft of the `admin` service used
  `${BOT_OPS_CONFIG_DIR:?required}` for its required vars, and a bare `docker compose up -d
  --build` for **the bot service alone** — no `--profile admin` anywhere — failed immediately on
  that error, since `admin`'s definition (unset vars and all) is still part of the same parse.
  The fix: a plain `${VAR:-}` default on `admin`'s own vars, with the *volume mount sources*
  falling back to an obviously-fake path (`/nonexistent-set-BOT_OPS_CONFIG_DIR`) instead of an
  empty string, so a misconfigured `--profile admin` run still fails clearly — from Docker
  itself at container-creation time, once `admin` is actually the profile being brought up —
  rather than breaking every other profile's `config`/`up` in the same file.
- **`ops/install.sh` resolves a `DEPLOY_UID`/`DEPLOY_GID` once, up front, rather than trusting
  `id -u`/`id -g` at each chown site.** `curl | bash -s -- prod` (the normal flow) runs entirely
  as the invoking non-root user — `sudo` is only invoked *internally*, by `bootstrap_dir`, for the
  one-time `/opt` escalation. But `curl | sudo bash -s -- prod` (piping the whole interpreter
  through `sudo`) makes `id -u` report 0 for the *entire* script, not just that one escalation, so
  every file it creates — not only what `bootstrap_dir` chowns — would otherwise end up
  root-owned. `SUDO_UID`/`SUDO_GID` (set by `sudo` itself, never the operator) name who actually
  invoked it; with no `sudo` in the picture at all there's no real user to hand ownership to, so
  install.sh refuses outright rather than deploying an instance only root can maintain (issue
  #54). Every chown site (`bootstrap_dir`, `fetch()`, the `STACK_ENV_TMP` write, `backups/`) uses
  the resolved `$DEPLOY_UID:$DEPLOY_GID` unconditionally — in the normal non-root path this just
  equals the current user's own ids, and chowning a file to its own owner is always a safe no-op.
- **`bin/bot-ops.sh` is shared per HOST, not per instance, so `install.sh <instance> <branch>`
  guards it before overwriting it (#230).** `/opt/rackbops-discord-bot/bin/bot-ops.sh` has no
  instance segment, and `docker-compose.yml`'s `admin` service mounts that one directory read-only
  into EVERY instance's admin container — so a per-instance branch argument that freely overwrote
  it would let a debug/feature deploy silently replace the script the PRODUCTION panel executes.
  `install_shared_bin()` compares the branch's own copy against **`main`'s**, never against
  whatever happens to already be installed: identical (or `BRANCH` is `main` itself), it installs
  and says so; different, it refuses — naming both `BOT_OPS_SCHEMA`s and the shared path — and
  leaves the installed file untouched unless `--force-bin` was passed. Comparing against `main`
  rather than the installed file is deliberate: it's what lets a stale install still refresh from a
  branch that ships `main`'s own script, while refusing exactly the case the issue describes. The
  #173/#178 schema-drift check (`ops/admin/server.ts`) was startup-only when #230 landed — a script
  swapped in by `--force-bin` (or a same-branch re-run) wasn't visible in the panel's "OUT OF DATE"
  banner until the admin container was recreated — and #230 declined the issue's alternative of
  making that check live, since it would leave the overwrite itself in place and only report it
  sooner. **#464 (A2) reverses that decline:** the banner is now re-checked about once a minute
  (`createOutdatedRefresher`), because a re-run of `install.sh` is the complete remedy for the
  `bot-ops.sh` half and a one-shot banner would stay stale right after the operator did what it says.
  The compose half still needs the admin container recreated (it mounts that one file and keeps the
  old inode; mounting the directory is #486's), through install.sh's printed step 4 with
  `--force-recreate` added.
- **`cloudflared`'s `TUNNEL_TOKEN` is sourced the same way `admin`'s secrets are — named-var
  Compose interpolation, exported by hand out of `$CONFIG_DIR/.env` — not `env_file:`.**
  `env_file: ${BOT_ENV_FILE:-.env}` would load *every* secret in the instance's `.env`
  (`DISCORD_TOKEN`, `BLIZZARD_CLIENT_SECRET`, `GITHUB_TOKEN`) into the `cloudflared` container's
  process environment wholesale, and would still need an entrypoint rewrite to rename
  `CLOUDFLARE_TUNNEL_TOKEN` to the `TUNNEL_TOKEN` name the `cloudflared` binary itself expects —
  the same anti-pattern the `admin` service's own comment already rejects for itself.
  `ops/install.sh`'s printed next-steps read the value out of `.env` with the same
  `grep '^KEY=' ... | cut -d= -f2-` one-liner already used for `ADMIN_TOKEN` (issue #54).
- **The redeploy order is inverted on purpose (nazumods/wow#879), and the reason is a kernel boundary.** Every
  "shut down, then have something bring the replacement up" variant dies on the same thing: a
  detached process (`setsid`, double fork, `Bun.spawn({ detached: true })`) leaves its *parent*, not
  the container's **PID namespace** or **cgroup**, and per `pid_namespaces(7)` the kernel `SIGKILL`s
  every process in a namespace when its PID 1 exits. `init: true` doesn't help either — tini exits
  with its main child. Starting the replacement *first* means there is no window to survive, so
  nothing has to. Don't "simplify" this back into an exit-then-respawn.
- **The old container is stopped by a daemon call, kept through probation, then removed (#548).**
  Under `restart: unless-stopped` a container that merely *exits* is respawned **on its old
  image** — the original nazumods/wow#868 failure, and now with two bots live. An explicit
  `docker stop` is exempt from that policy, which is why retirement is a daemon call rather than a
  process exit, and why the stopped original stays down while it is kept as the rollback target.
  `completeTakeOver` removes it once the new build has run its first tick; a `docker start` of it
  before then is a rollback, and the replacement steps aside.
- **A running original after the stop is a rollback — #548.** Because the original is kept until
  probation ends, `docker start <name>` of it is the operator choosing the old build. The
  replacement looks at four points: at boot, right after `resolveBootMode()` and before login
  (`index.ts`); at the top of `takeOver`, before `ready` is written; in `completeTakeOver`, before
  it tags; and again after a refused removal. The first two looks are `detectRollback()`: the
  original is RUNNING and this container's own restart policy is not `no` (an empty name counts
  as `no`). A first attempt's standby is created with `no`, so a live original beside it is the
  ordinary handoff, never a rollback — and a standby from before #160 carries no
  `HANDOFF_RESTART_POLICY` in its env, so it is never read as one either; an original whose own
  policy was `no` is restored as `no`, so those two looks never fire for it. The two in
  `completeTakeOver` ask only whether the original is running (by then this container has taken
  over, so no policy check is needed). Stepping aside means `stepAside()` stops THIS container through the daemon (an
  explicit stop is exempt from `unless-stopped`, so it stays down as an exited `<name>-next`);
  only if that call fails, or no SIGTERM arrives within `STEP_ASIDE_GRACE_MS` (30 s), does it
  `exit(1)`. The safe operator form is `docker stop <name>-next` first and `docker start <name>`
  second (README: "The original is stopped at the handoff but kept"): a bare `docker start`
  leaves two bots until the replacement's next look, and that stop's SIGTERM starts a drain in
  which the first tick can settle, which is why `completeTakeOver` returns early under `isShuttingDown()` instead of
  completing the swap while it drains. What the looks cannot see: a `recreate`/`env-set` during
  probation gives the original a NEW container id that none of them follow — `bot-ops.sh` now
  refuses both (and `restart`) while `<name>-next` is alive, #490; a plain `compose up -d` has no
  guard, and while it most likely just starts the stopped original (a rollback the replacement
  steps aside for), a forced recreate it triggered could leave two bots (unverified without a
  daemon, as is whether compose also recreates `<name>-next`); a replacement that hangs rather
  than crashes never gets as far as a look, so stop it first (`docker stop <name>-next`, or
  `docker rm -f <name>-next` to end it at once) and only then `docker start <name>`; with `AUTO_UPDATE=true` a revived
  original can update itself to the bad build again; and a redeploy started inside the
  replacement's first tick may not converge (#547).
- **`GIT_SHA` is stripped from the replacement's copied env** (`buildCreateSpec`). Docker's
  `Config.Env` merges the image's baked ENV with the container's own, so copying it verbatim pins
  the *old* sha onto the new container and overrides the new image's — a bot that updated correctly
  would then report its own update as a ⚠️ no-op.
- **A plain `compose up -d` after a swap recreates the container — expected, not a bug.** The
  replacement copies the original's labels verbatim, including `com.docker.compose.config-hash`,
  which embeds the `GIT_SHA` build-arg environment of whatever `up` created the original — so
  compose sees a stale hash and recreates. Benign, verified on the box: compose recognises the
  replacement as its own service (no second bot — the trap the labels exist to prevent), and the
  recreate reuses `:latest`, which *is* the self-deployed build, so no rollback — true
  unconditionally only since issue #39: `:latest` is applied by `completeTakeOver()` only *after* the
  replacement has verified and run through probation (#548), never at build time, so a build that
  merely compiled but crashed at boot, or logged in and then died in `activate()`, can no longer
  leave `:latest` pointing at it. Don't "fix" the config-hash mismatch itself by
  computing a fresh hash — a wrong guess would make compose orphan the service and start a second
  container, the far worse failure.
- **The standby attaches nothing** until it has taken over: both instances hold the same
  `DISCORD_TOKEN` and Discord delivers every event to **both** gateway sessions, so a standby that
  registered handlers would double every command reply for the length of the overlap.
- **The handoff marker is its own file, not `state.json`.** The replacement writes it
  (`writeMarker`, a plain `Bun.write`: a half-written marker reads as `undefined` through
  `readMarker`'s tolerant parse, so it needs no temp+rename) and clears it once it has taken over
  (`takeOver`, once the original is stopped — the marker's job ends there, not at the end of
  probation); the original reads it, and clears it right before it creates the replacement and after a failed
  one (`redeploy.ts`). One writer in the normal case (from two sites), two clearers, and a survivor's writes ignored, not prevented (each marker names the container that wrote it by its short id, when it has one, #506). `decideHandoffOutcome` reads it *before* the container state because a
  replacement that signalled `ready` is already stopping the original, so it can be caught mid-stop
  and would otherwise be misread as one that died.
- **`bot-ops.sh env-set` must never gain `--build`.** It recreates without building so it reuses
  the `<project>-bot:latest` tag a self-update wrote once the new build finished probation (#548;
  until then `:latest` is still the previous build, and a recreate would give the stopped original a
  new container id that nothing follows — which is why `bot-ops.sh` refuses `env-set`, `recreate`
  and `restart` while `<name>-next` is alive, #490); adding `--build`
  would rebuild from whatever the box's checkout is on, silently rolling the bot back on every
  settings edit.
- **`bot-ops.sh env-set` diffs before it validates a value's format, and format-validates only
  what changes; `env-get` and that diff read `.env` with compose's `env_file` semantics (issue
  #44).** Whitelist *membership* is still checked for every submitted key regardless of whether it
  changes — that's authority, not format. Don't "tighten" the format check back to running over
  every submitted line: a stored value the bot accepts but a whitelist regex rejects (a
  hand-quoted value, a CRLF-saved file, `1, 2` in `ADMIN_USER_IDS`) then fails every save that
  echoes it back, naming a key nobody touched — and the admin panel used to echo every field. `load_env_values` reads the file the way compose does (checked against `docker compose
  config`): last occurrence wins, `export KEY=` and indented lines count, whitespace/CR and one
  layer of matching quotes are stripped — so the panel shows, and diffs against, the value the bot
  actually runs with (not modelled: inline `#` comments, `${VAR}` interpolation, spaces around `=`,
  a `KEY: value` separator, escapes inside double quotes — nothing `env-set` writes can produce
  them). The rewrite still preserves every unchanged line verbatim (a missing final newline is
  added); only an indented or `export`ed line for a key being changed comes back as plain `KEY=`.
  `ops/bot-ops.test.ts` pins all of it with the real script, and the panel side (`applyPending`
  posts only the changed keys, composed by `planApply` from `planEnvSave` and `planPluginsSave`) is
  pinned by `ops/admin/server.test.ts`, which lifts those functions from the page source and runs
  `applyPending` against a stubbed page.
- **The bot container *starts* as root but *runs* as `bun`** (`user: "0:0"` in
  `docker-compose.yml` + `entrypoint.sh`). The daemon socket is mode 0660 `root:docker` and the
  docker GID differs per host (115 on the box, 999 on a stock Debian) — naming one would be the
  operator-supplied config nazumods/wow#879 rules out — so the entrypoint reads the GID off the socket
  itself (`stat -c %g`), then `setpriv`s to `bun` carrying that one supplementary group. Hygiene,
  not a boundary: the socket mount is already root-equivalent either way. Under the image's own
  `USER bun` (no socket, older compose file) it execs the CMD untouched. State written by a
  pre-drop root-run deployment stays root-owned — a one-time `chown -R bun:bun` on the host's
  `data/`, not something the entrypoint redoes every start.
- **`HANDOFF_FROM` alone is not enough to arm standby (#46).** It's baked into the replacement's
  env at creation time and container env is immutable, so nothing ever clears it — a swapped-in
  container carries its now-dead original's id forever. Without `resolveBootMode()` confirming the
  named original still *exists* on the daemon, every later in-place restart of that container
  (`bot-ops.sh restart`, a host reboot, a daemon restart, a crash loop under `unless-stopped`; not
  the ops panel's Restart, which since #277 is a `recreate` — compose `--force-recreate`, a fresh container
  with no `HANDOFF_FROM` at all) would re-arm standby against an id that's long gone — a benign detour in
  the ordinary case, but an outage in exactly the degraded conditions (slow gateway login, a
  hung/refusing daemon socket) where a plain boot would have recovered.
  - **The check is existence (a 404), deliberately not the original's live running state.** A
    running-state check was tried and reverted: a container that merely hasn't been (re)started
    yet post-reboot still exists (`docker inspect` still returns 200, `State.Running: false`), so
    keying on running-state instead of existence would race a host reboot mid-handoff — both
    containers restart under `unless-stopped` in no guaranteed order, and if the replacement's own
    check landed in the window before the daemon had gotten around to restarting the original, it
    would misread a genuinely-in-flight handoff as "original gone," go live immediately, and never
    retire it — two live bots answering the same Discord token. Existence doesn't have that
    window: a container's object persists across a reboot regardless of whether it's currently
    running.
  - **A stopped-but-not-removed original stays `standby`.** Since #548 this is the normal state
    during probation: `retireOriginal` only stops the original, and `completeTakeOver` removes it
    (best-effort) once the new build has run its first tick. Until then the corpse holds the
    canonical name and the replacement stays `<name>-next` (no rename is attempted before the
    removal); after a failed removal the replacement's own rename fails alongside it (name
    conflict, caught non-fatally) — it is left visibly un-renamed, non-fatal but not where
    `bot-ops.sh` looks. `resolveBootMode` re-arming
    standby on that container's later restarts is a narrower version of the pre-#46 bug (it needs
    the corpse to still be there, versus every restart unconditionally before), and it's retried,
    not stuck: the restarted container is a standby beside the kept original, so its `takeOver`
    re-runs the same `retireOriginal` and its probation re-runs `completeTakeOver`, whose removal
    may well succeed this time. (That is also how a swap interrupted by `docker stop <name>-next`
    completes if the container is started again — unless the operator started the original, which
    `detectRollback` reads as a rollback and steps aside for.) That retry's own `stopContainer`
    call is handled, not left to propagate — see the `retireOriginal` gotcha below for how it
    tells a genuine first attempt (original still alive, so a stop failure must reach the
    original to reclaim) from a call against an original with nothing left to reclaim it. The mirror case, an original that is
    already GONE with the rename never landed, boots `normal`; `index.ts` then runs `healOwnName()` (#547),
    which takes the canonical name back when it is free or held by a stopped container of the same compose
    service.
  - **`-next` is a reserved suffix, and nothing is ever force-removed by name** (#492, #547). `baseName()`
    strips it, `replacementName()` is always `<own name>-next` (`ownName()`: the compose project when the
    container is that or `<project>-next`, so an install.sh instance installed before #567 with a name like `canary-next` keeps its
    own name (a new one is refused) and self-updates into `<name>-next`, e.g. `rackbops-discord-bot-prod-next-next`; otherwise the base), and `redeploy()`
    refuses to run from a bot that is not at its own name, i.e. one that is itself `<name>-next`: with
    `HANDOFF_FROM` set, "this bot is named `<name>-next`, which is a swap's replacement name: if the swap that
    started it is still finishing, wait a few minutes; if it persists, the rename never landed and `docker restart
    <name>-next` heals the name at boot (#547). The automatic check will not retry this sha, so run /update
    again afterwards" (a refusal leaves `attemptedUpdateToSha` set, and
    `bot-ops.sh restart` is refused by the swap guard in this very state, so the docker command is the one
    that works); without it, "this container's name ends in -next, which the self-update reserves for its
    replacement, so it cannot self-update: give it another container_name and recreate it". The leftover check is a post-build inspect
    outside the quiesce: a RUNNING holder of the replacement's name ends the redeploy ("a running container
    already holds `<name>-next`"), a stopped one is removed by id, un-forced, so one started in between is a
    409 and a reported failure, never a kill. A compose-created container an operator renames to `*-next` by
    hand is not healed at boot (the heal needs `HANDOFF_FROM`, so that an ordinary boot never touches the
    socket): `docker rename <name>-next <name>`.
  - **An unconfirmed daemon error — including a timeout — stays `standby`, never downgrades.** A
    genuine handoff boot needs a live daemon connection for the rest of the protocol regardless, so
    treating "can't tell" as "gone" risks two live bots on the shared token, same reasoning as the
    running-state case above. `docker.ts` bounds every exported call in its own `bounded()`
    wrapper, spanning the request AND the response-body read (`DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_MS`, 60s — #130;
    deliberately *not* at the `api()` funnel, which takes whatever signal it is handed, so the
    bound can cover a body read that happens after the headers land); the one daemon call
    `resolveBootMode` makes keeps a
    *tighter* 10s bound (`RESOLVE_BOOT_MODE_TIMEOUT_MS`) because it's the first daemon dependency
    `index.ts` has *before* `client.login()`, so a hung socket there falls through to "can't
    confirm" without eating 60s of boot before a login is even attempted.
- **`ready` is a promise of a stop, not the stop itself.** The replacement writes the marker
  *before* retiring the original, so a `retireOriginal` that dies in between would leave the
  original quiesced forever — seen `ready`, stopped counting, waiting to be killed. Two guards
  close that: the original bounds the wait for its own death (`RETIREMENT_DEADLINE_MS`, outcome
  `stalled` — reclaim, remove the replacement, report "check the daemon socket"), and past the
  `stopContainer` point of no return nothing throws: `retireOriginal` ends at the stop, and
  `completeTakeOver`, which does the rest, is best-effort and logs instead, because the
  original is dead by then (it can no longer reclaim; since #548 an operator's `docker start`, a
  compose `up -d`/`restart`, or `restart: always` after a daemon restart can revive it, and that
  is a rollback the replacement steps aside for) and a rejecting `ClientReady` would strand *both*
  containers.
- **`retireOriginal` can run against an original with nothing left alive to reclaim it — only a
  genuinely *live* original gets the strict `stopContainer` failure handling.** Two separate paths
  land here with the original already gone in some sense: (1) the original is a
  stopped-but-not-removed corpse that still *exists* — by design during probation since #548, or
  after a `completeTakeOver` removal that failed — and a later
  standby boot (raw env `bootMode`, or #46's daemon-confirmed `resolveBootMode`, whichever is
  current) retries `takeOver` -> `retireOriginal` against that same id; (2) `HANDOFF_FROM` never
  clears from a swapped-in container's own env (it's baked in at creation, and container env is
  immutable), so any boot path that still honors it against an id since fully removed — not just a
  corpse, gone entirely — lands here too. `stopContainer` already tolerates the expected 304
  (already stopped) / 404 (already gone), so the only way either call fails is a genuine daemon
  error — and on a genuine first attempt that must still propagate (the original is alive,
  watching, and reclaims at its deadline), but in both of the above there is no live original left
  to reclaim, so propagating would just crash the sole live bot via `takeOver`'s catch ->
  `exit(1)`, an `unless-stopped`-recoverable but wholly self-inflicted restart. `retireOriginal`
  tells them apart by inspecting the original *before* stopping it: running means a genuine first
  attempt (strict — let a stop failure propagate); anything else — not running, or gone entirely
  (`tryInspectContainer` returns `undefined`) — means nothing is left to reclaim (degrade a stop
  failure to a warning rather than throw). An earlier version of this fix only covered the
  not-running case and missed `undefined`, which is in practice the *more* commonly reached of the
  two — caught in review before merging.
- **The standby is created with `RestartPolicy: {Name:"no"}`, never the original's — #160.** The
  original's real policy (`self.HostConfig.RestartPolicy?.Name || "unless-stopped"`) rides along
  instead, in the env (`HANDOFF_RESTART_POLICY_ENV`, stripped-then-reset exactly like
  `HANDOFF_FROM_ENV` so it can't stack across a chain of standbys; for `on-failure` the cap rides
  beside it in `HANDOFF_RESTART_RETRIES_ENV` so the restore keeps `on-failure:3` rather than
  becoming unlimited retries, #490). **`takeOver` restores it onto itself AFTER the `ready` marker
  is written and BEFORE `retire(originalId)` — that order is the whole fix, not an
  implementation detail.** #130 bounds `stopContainer` at 60s, so a stop the daemon genuinely
  performed but answered slowly is now indistinguishable, from the replacement's side, from one
  that never happened — if the restore ran only *after* that stop, the window between "original
  stopped" and "policy restored" would have BOTH containers down with neither's restart policy
  able to bring either back: a permanent outage, no race required. Restoring first means a throw
  at the stop is caught before the original is touched, and the original — still alive — reclaims
  normally. Never `{Name:"on-failure", MaximumRetryCount:0}` as an alternative to `"no"` — Docker
  reads `0` as *unlimited* retries, not zero; a trap, not a middle ground. `restorePolicy` is
  deliberately **not** best-effort like `completeTakeOver`'s tag, removal and rename — a swallowed failure would leave the
  *surviving* bot on `RestartPolicy:"no"` forever, silently, discovered only when a host reboot
  doesn't bring it back weeks later. Its own `exit(1)` (via `takeOver`'s outer catch) is **not**
  the `unless-stopped`-recoverable case the gotcha above describes for a *retire* failure — ON A
  FIRST ATTEMPT this container's own policy is still `"no"` when this failure fires (it's the very
  thing that failed to get restored), so recovery there is the ORIGINAL's job: still live, still
  polling (this is the only path that reaches this exit with the original genuinely alive — see the
  ⚠️ design-constraint section on #160), it reclaims via its own `RETIREMENT_DEADLINE_MS`/
  failed-marker path exactly as if the replacement had never signalled at all. A RE-ENTRANT
  `takeOver` (the "nothing left to reclaim" gotcha above — a stale `HANDOFF_FROM` re-arming standby
  on a later restart of a container that already retired successfully once) is different: this
  container's policy was already restored to the ORIGINAL's real value the first time around, so
  it is *not* still `"no"` here, and `unless-stopped` (or whatever that real value was) recovers it
  regardless of whether the stale `originalId` still names anything live — and if it names a
  RUNNING container (the operator rolled back with `docker start`), the restarted replacement
  steps aside instead of stopping it again (`detectRollback`, #548). Don't read "recovery is
  the original's job" as universal — it's the first-attempt case; the retry case recovers by the
  policy already being right from before.
- **A stop that *throws* during `retireOriginal`'s live branch is no longer automatically a failed
  stop — #160.** Before the standby's own policy could be `"no"`, any `stopContainer` failure on a
  live original was unambiguous: propagate, let the still-alive original reclaim. Post-#160, #130's
  60s bound means a stop the daemon actually performed but answered slowly looks identical to one
  that never ran — and treating it as a failure would exit the replacement (now the only bot that
  can go live) over what was in fact success. The fix re-inspects on any throw: original confirmed
  gone or not running → the stop worked, log and go on exactly as the ordinary path would (the
  removal and rename are no longer `retireOriginal`'s: `completeTakeOver` does them after
  probation, #548); original still confirmed running → the stop genuinely failed, rethrow, unchanged.
  Only the live branch gets this — the already-stopped/already-gone branches already tolerated a
  stop failure outright (see the gotcha above) and don't need a re-inspect to tell why.
- **`redeploy()`'s pre-removal generation check compares the replacement's `State.StartedAt`
  against when its outcome was decided, not just whether it's currently running — #160, defence in
  depth.** Change 2 above already stops Docker itself from resurrecting an unverified standby, so
  this covers what's left: a manual `docker start` on a removed-but-still-present replacement, a
  standby a pre-#160 original created (still on `unless-stopped`), or `restorePolicy`'s own
  transport-drop mirror case (see `takeOver`'s docstring) genuinely restarting the replacement mid
  retirement-wait (since #548 such a restarted replacement, finding the original running while its own
  policy is already restored, steps aside through `detectRollback` instead of re-running the standby
  cycle; the original, alive, then reclaims on its own). `Date.parse(current.State.StartedAt) > observedAt` means a *different life* of
  the same container id — force-removing it would kill a bot that may be genuinely up and serving. A
  tie (same instant) is not `>` and is removed, matching the ordinary case exactly (nothing races
  its own `StartedAt` against itself). **`observedAt` is NOT always `awaitHandoff`'s own timestamp**
  — that's true only for `failed`/`timeout` decided directly from a poll. When `ready` is later
  demoted to `stalled` or `failed` (the retirement wait, up to `RETIREMENT_DEADLINE_MS` — 3 real
  minutes — after "ready" first arrived), `redeploy()` re-samples `observedAt` at THAT decision
  instead: an unrefreshed baseline pinned to the stale "ready" moment would wrongly read a
  replacement that restarted anywhere in that 3-minute window as "a later generation," silently
  skipping a removal it should make (found in review; `redeploy.test.ts`'s dedicated "between
  'ready' and the LATE stalled decision" test is the mutation guard). It is also re-sampled in the backstop catch, and its pre-poll default is a real time that no current path reads (the poll's return and the catch both assign first), so it is never epoch 0 (#431): that default used to read EVERY running
  replacement as a later life and skip the removal after a poll error. A failed poll inspect no longer reaches that catch at all (#433): `awaitHandoff` catches it per poll, a failed inspect
  is no verdict, and a `ready` the replacement wrote after that poll's marker read is picked up by `redeploy()`'s re-read before any other verdict is acted on (a `failed` written then is reported as `failed`, with its own reason). A confirmation inspect that
  itself fails must NOT skip the removal — that would be a new, silent way to leak a replacement
  container on an ordinary daemon blip; only a *positive* confirmed later generation ever skips it.
- **A `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` handler now drains in-flight work before exiting — #154, the first signal
  handler in this codebase.** `beginHandoff()` doesn't itself await a tick already running (its own
  docstring says so) — the drain is `shutdown.ts`'s job, triggered by the `SIGTERM` a `docker stop`
  sends (an operator's, a compose restart, or #160's own `retireOriginal`). **Plugin ticks (#217, #248):**
  a plugin tick that overruns `PLUGIN_TICK_TIMEOUT_MS` is no longer waited on by its scheduler tick,
  but the call holds its own unit of the critical section until it settles. A restart request or a
  shutdown (`restart.ts`'s `onStopRequested`) aborts every pending tick's `AbortSignal` and lets go
  of each hold after `PLUGIN_TICK_ABORT_GRACE_MS` (5 s) at most, so a cooperating tick bails before
  the exit and one that ignores its signal can't hold a restart forever (`restart.ts`'s header). **The re-entrancy
  problem this solves: a straight "wait for `critical === 0`" deadlocks the auto-update path**,
  where `redeploy()` runs *inside* the very tick's `withCritical` section (`announce.ts`'s
  `onTick` → `guardedTick` → `withCritical` → `runTick` → `autoUpdate` → `applyUpdate` →
  `redeploy`) that would be waiting on itself. **The fix is a live exemption, not a frozen
  baseline** (`restart.ts`'s `handoffExemption()`): while a handoff is active, exactly 1 unit of
  `critical` depth is exempted — by construction the handoff's own holder (the tick, or a
  `/update` interaction: `announce.ts`'s `onTick` (its `withCritical` wraps the whole tick) and
  `commands.ts`'s `/update` handler (a `withCritical` around `checkForUpdate`) are the only callers of
  `checkForUpdate`, the only way `redeploy()` is reached), which structurally can never
  close on its own while the handoff is in progress (`redeploy()` never returns on a successful
  handoff — the replacement kills this process mid-await). `awaitCriticalIdle` waits for `critical`
  to fall back to (or below) that live exemption. No handoff at all (a plain `docker stop`,
  `bot-ops.sh restart`, compose down) means exemption 0, i.e. wait for full idle. **An earlier
  version of this fix snapshotted `critical` at `beginHandoff()` time instead** (a "baseline") —
  found in review to be wrong whenever a genuinely unrelated critical section (a concurrently-open
  scheduler tick) happened to also be open at that instant: the snapshot baked its depth in too, so
  the drain reported "idle" immediately even though that tick's own write was still in flight,
  exactly the bug #154 exists to fix. A live "exempt exactly the one holder" has no such window,
  whatever else opens or closes and in whatever order.
- **The drain cannot delay a handoff past `RETIREMENT_DEADLINE_MS` (3 minutes) — the numbers don't
  allow it.** `SHUTDOWN_GRACE_MS` is 8 seconds total — the drain AND `destroyClient` together, see
  the next bullet — chosen to leave real margin under `stopContainer`'s own `t=10` (`docker.ts`) and
  compose's 10s SIGKILL default — **not** raised to make more room, since that's #160's territory
  (lengthens every handoff) not this one's. 8s is a small fraction of 180s (22.5×, a bit over one
  order of magnitude): even a drain that runs out its full grace before the daemon SIGKILLs the
  process is well under the deadline the *retirement* wait (`awaitRetirementFailure`) tolerates.
- **`shutdown.ts`'s drain and its `destroyClient` call are NESTED inside one `SHUTDOWN_GRACE_MS`
  budget, not sequential.** `destroyClient` is bounded to `min(DESTROY_CLIENT_TIMEOUT_MS,
  remaining time in graceMs after the drain)` — found in review that an earlier version gave
  `destroyClient` a fresh `DESTROY_CLIENT_TIMEOUT_MS` (2s) *on top of* the full drain budget (8s),
  an additive worst case of 10s that exactly tied (rather than beat) the 10s SIGKILL bound.
- **A shutdown owns the exit; a pending restart no longer pre-empts the drain (#512).** `endCritical`
  used to call `doExit` before `maybeResolveIdle`, so a SIGTERM draining while a restart was pending
  exited 75 the moment that section closed and `shutdown.ts`'s handler never reached
  `disposePlugins`/`destroyClient`. Once `beginShutdown()` has run, `endCritical()` and `requestRestart()`
  do not exit (and `beginShutdown` disarms the restart's deferral deadline): the section's close releases
  the drain and the handler exits 0, also after a drain that times out. Exit 0, not 75 — a SIGTERM means
  something external wants the process gone. A requested restart on its own still does not run `dispose()`
  or close the gateway: `contract.ts` documents `dispose()` as running "on the way out" and lists a
  `docker stop`, a self-update's retire and SIGINT (a requested restart is not on that list), routing exit
  75 through the shutdown sequence would add a second dispose path and a wait behind the drain, and
  process exit frees the resources (#512 item 4's declined half).
- **#184 added a THIRD nested phase — `disposePlugins`, between the drain and `destroyClient` —
  and each phase's remaining-budget calculation must measure elapsed time from the SIGNAL, not
  from whichever phase happened to run immediately before it.** `destroyClient`'s bound uses
  `Date.now() - startedAt` (total elapsed since the signal), not the drain's own `elapsedMs` —
  using the latter would let `destroyClient` believe it has more of the budget left than it
  actually does whenever `disposePlugins` itself took real time. A test asserting only the
  three-phases-together total (`shutdown.test.ts`) didn't catch this on its own — the per-plugin
  and per-phase timeouts already kept the total small enough either way — so a SECOND, narrower
  test (a `disposePlugins` fake that resolves ON ITS OWN partway through its bound, not via the
  bound itself) was needed to isolate it. `plugins/host.ts`'s own `disposePlugins(loaded, log,
  timeoutMs)` adds a FOURTH, per-plugin layer inside that: each plugin's own `dispose()` gets
  `PLUGIN_DISPOSE_TIMEOUT_MS`, so one wedged plugin can't block the rest — **critically, this
  requires disposing every running plugin CONCURRENTLY (`Promise.allSettled`), not one after
  another.** A sequential first version (found in review) let N plugins' timeouts sum — at N=2 that
  already exceeds `DISPOSE_PLUGINS_TIMEOUT_MS`'s own 3s outer bound, so the outer bound could fire
  and the process could exit mid-way through a LATER plugin's dispose, having never given it a real
  chance to run. Concurrent dispose makes N plugins cost the same worst case as one, so the outer
  bound stays a fixed, small backstop regardless of how many plugins are installed — not a value
  that would need to grow with the plugin count.
- **`writeJsonAtomic`'s temp name is unique per (process TOKEN, call) since #253 — a pid+counter
  name ALONE (#154's original fix, `${path}.${pid}.${counter}.tmp`) is not enough, and #253 found
  the gap `#154` left.** The concrete hazard: the replacement's boot-time `loadPluginIndex` cache
  write (`index.ts`, before `takeOver` — so before the "the standby writes nothing until it has
  taken over" invariant `state.json` relies on even applies) and the original's still-running
  `pluginUpdates` tick re-fetching the same manifest are two DIFFERENT PROCESSES that can both write
  `data/plugins/index.json` during a handoff — a fixed temp name let either process's rename fail
  with `ENOENT` out from under the other. #154's per-(process, call) name fixed that for two
  processes in different pid namespaces, but the bot runs under an init (`docker-compose.yml`'s
  `init: true`), so the original and its replacement — two DIFFERENT containers, each its own pid
  namespace — are very likely the SAME small pid, both starting their own in-memory `tmpCounter` at
  zero: their first writes to a shared path picked the identical "unique" name, the exact collision
  #154 was supposed to rule out. `TMP_TOKEN` (`storage.ts`, `randomBytes(4).toString("hex")`, chosen
  once per process at module load) is what actually separates two processes whose pid AND counter
  both happen to match — `tmpPathFor(path, pid, token, counter)` is the one exported pure function
  the guarantee runs through, testable without two real processes. `src/plugins/index.ts` used to
  keep its own copy of the atomic-write pattern, which got #154's fix by hand but not #253's (#253's
  PR scoped itself to `storage.ts`), so `data/plugins/index.json` alone kept the pid+counter name
  through the handoff. Since #496 it writes through `storage.ts`'s `writeFileAtomic` — the same
  `tmpPathFor` name `writeJsonAtomic` uses, for text that is already serialized — so the token covers
  it too; `storage.ts` imports only `node:*` and the `node:path`-only leaf `./dataDir`, so `src/plugins/*` stays free of `config.ts`.
  In-process races are unaffected either way — `createJsonWriter`/`createKeyedJsonMutator` still own
  that, unchanged.
- **A second `/update` mid-swap answers `busy`** — the guard sits at the top of
  `checkForUpdate`, before any state write or network call, because interaction handling does
  not quiesce during a handoff (only the scheduler does) and `/update`'s `force: true` bypasses
  the anti-loop suppression: unguarded, it would overwrite the in-flight attempt's
  `pendingUpdateReport` and then force-remove its replacement. Since #449 the guard is
  `restartPending() || checkInFlight`: a pending exit-75 restart (the no-socket path, deferred behind
  the command's own critical section) refuses a second `/update` too, which used to overwrite the
  first requester's `pendingUpdateReport`.
- **The guard is two flags, not one (#51 item 4; the first is `restartPending()` since #449).** `handoffActive()` alone doesn't read true
  until `redeploy()` calls `beginHandoff()` just before creating the replacement (#130 moved it
  past the build) — so a second call landing in the stretch before that (both GitHub fetches,
  `saveState()`, `redeployAvailable()`'s daemon ping, and now the build, all async) used to pass
  straight through. `checkInFlight`, a module-level flag in `update.ts` claimed synchronously
  before the first `await` and released in `finally`, closes that window:
  two calls fired back-to-back (no `await` between them) can never both observe it as false.
- **`/report`** (`src/report.ts`) is disabled unless BOTH `REPORT_ROLE_ID` and `GITHUB_TOKEN`
  are set (it replies "not configured" otherwise). `project` is a fixed choices list, so
  `handleReportCommand` only ever builds a modal `customId` (`report:<project>`) for a real
  project — but the submit handler can't lean on that alone (a forged `modal_submit` interaction
  can carry any `customId`), so `handleReportModal` looks the project up via
  `config.ts`'s `repoForProject`, which uses `Object.hasOwn` rather than a bracket lookup: on a
  plain object, `REPORT_PROJECTS[project]` resolves inherited `Object.prototype` keys too, so
  `"constructor"`/`"__proto__"`/`"toString"` returned truthy and reached `createIssue` before this
  fix (#55, #186). With the fence closed, an unknown project genuinely can't reach the handler —
  it's refused at `if (!repo)` instead. Role check
  reads `member.roles` from the interaction payload (cached manager **or** raw `string[]`), so no
  privileged Members intent is needed; no `member` (a DM) holds no role, and the command says so in words
  (`REPORT_DM_REFUSAL`, #504) rather than with the role text. **The submit re-checks configuration
  and role too (#448)**, before `deferReply` and before any network call, refusing ephemerally with the
  command's texts (the not-configured one naming the prefixed command, since a submit carries no
  `commandName`; a submit with no `member` gets the role text — the DM wording is the command's): it is defence in depth, because the code cannot verify that Discord scopes a submission to
  the interaction that showed it, and whether a roleless account can forge one is left unverified. It also
  closes the old race of a role revoked between showing the modal and submitting it. **Filing is three steps
  with three outcomes (#445):** `ensureLabel` first and best-effort — it returns whether the label exists
  afterwards (201 created, or 422, which with a constant name and colour reads as "already there") and never
  throws, so a label that cannot be created or checked (another status, a network error, the 10 s timeout)
  files the issue **without** it instead of failing the report (#503 item 7; the issue then carries no
  `automated` label, and the log says so); then `createIssue` in its own `try`, the only step whose failure
  says "Couldn't file the issue" (nothing is *known* to have been filed: a create that timed out, or answered
  with a body that could not be read, may still have committed, and a retry would then file it twice); then the announcement edit in its own `try` — if that fails the issue
  exists, so it is logged and a minimal public edit names the issue, and if that fails too it is logged at
  error level and nothing else is attempted (the deferred reply expires). **Refusals and failures on the
  submit path write a `[report]` log line** (#503 item 11, the `/report` half): the two gate refusals name the
  user id and the project; the label shortfall, a create-issue failure and a failed announcement or fallback
  edit name the repo (and the issue number) — never the title, the description or the username. Not logged
  there: the unknown-project refusal (its string is attacker-controlled until `repoForProject` has accepted
  it) and anything that throws out of the handler (a rejected `deferReply`, the "Couldn't file" edit itself),
  which the dispatcher logs as an `[interaction]` error.
- **A `/report` outcome is public; its refusals are not (nazumods/wow#870).** The modal submit defers
  **without** `MessageFlags.Ephemeral`, so the filed-issue confirmation (and the failure that
  replaces it) lands in the channel the report came from — the transparency is the feature, and the
