  Apply are `busy` in the pending and failed views and `applyPending` / `discardPending` return at once.
  `allSettled`, not `all`: `all` rejects on the FIRST rejection, which would clear the flag while the other
  list was still loading. Each loader is started inside an async arrow so one that throws SYNCHRONOUSLY becomes
  its own rejection instead of skipping the other loader. Until the reads land the controls still hold the
  edits that are about to be dropped, so an Apply pressed then would send them: after a Discard it sent the
  very edits just discarded, and after a re-baselining failure the bar offered Apply for the length of the
  re-read. OK stays usable, and a kept-edits refusal (#272) starts no re-read, so it never locks the bar.
  `loadStatus()` is not part of it.
  **`aria-busy` on `#apply-bar` is set only in the PENDING view, never in the failed or done view**
  (`applyBarView`'s `ariaBusy`, written by `writeApplyBar`). Both of the latter write their message into the
  bar's `role="status"` region in the SAME task the re-read starts, and `aria-busy` on a container is
  specifically licensed to suppress that region's announcements until it clears -- marking the bar busy there
  risks the failure or success message going unheard (not verified here: no screen reader was at hand). The
  pending view has no terminal message to lose.
  **`reloadConfig()`'s three GETs share one 30 s timeout (`RELOAD_TIMEOUT_MS`, #282).** Before it, a hung
  `/api/plugins`, `/api/env` or `/api/env-schema` blocked Discard and Apply for as long as the request was
  outstanding, up to the server's own limits (`SUBPROCESS_TIMEOUT_MS = 90_000`, `IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 120`,
  `ops/admin/server.ts:592-593`) — and, because `reloadConfig` is single-flight, every later reload (Unlock's
  included) joined the hung one. Now a timed-out plugins or env read ends in `reloadConfig`'s `catch`, which
  writes "the server did not answer within 30 seconds" into both lists, and its `finally` clears the timer
  and the single-flight promise, so the bar unlocks and the next reload fetches afresh; a hung
  `/api/env-schema` alone falls back to `{}` like any other schema failure. `loadBranches()` keeps its own
  5 s timeout, before the 30 s one starts. **When the re-read ends, `rereadFromServer()` itself calls
  `settleFocus()` (#284)**: OK pressed during a failure's re-read puts focus on the bar, and a landing that
  leaves nothing pending hides the bar, which used to drop focus to `<body>`.
- **A request file that may carry a webhook URL is deleted on rejection, never moved to
  `requests/rejected/` (#241).** A webhook URL is a secret, and `rejected/` is a folder nobody treats
  as one and nothing ever prunes. The drain decides a file may carry one from its NAME (the writer
  names a file for its action, so `webhook-add`), from its TEXT (`webhooks/<id>/<token>`, with or
  without a host, so a port, a doubled slash, a trailing dot or no host at all does not hide it -- after
  every JSON unicode escape, backslash-slash and percent-escape is read, once, as the character it
  stands for; a doubly-encoded spelling is not chased) and from
  what it PARSES as. For such a file a rejection deletes it and its reason carries no parser text
  (`unreadable JSON` alone); an unparseable file that is not secret keeps the parser's message with its
  quoted text taken out (Bun quotes a bare identifier, which could be a token). An update request
  whose text holds a URL is refused and deleted outright: it never legitimately has one, and its
  `requestedBy` would be stored in `state.json`. A bare token with no URL around it cannot be told from
  any other text, so its file is set aside like any unparseable file -- what must not happen is a log
  line quoting it. If a delete fails the error names the file and nothing else. The file that carries
  an accepted URL sits in the mailbox for about one drain interval (five seconds) at the container's
  default mode before the bot deletes it: accepted, because shortening it belongs to the writer
  (`bot-ops.sh`); it is there longer if the bot cannot delete it (the log says so, the request is not
  applied again, and the delete is retried on every drain) or if the drain is busy ahead of it (a webhook
  lookup can take 10 s, and a half-written file costs one 250 ms wait). A bug in this area would show up as a token in a log line, a reason, a
  result, a temp file or `rejected/`: `secrets-leak.test.ts` searches all of them.
- **`routing.json`'s `results` is how the panel learns the outcome of a request (#241).** The bot
  appends `{ id, action, plugin?, channelId?, ok, reason?, at }` under the `id` the panel chose, keeps the
  newest 20, and the panel learns the outcome by reading the file, so it polls for its own id; a
  request with no valid `id` records nothing, and a refusal that failed to parse is still recorded under
  its id. An id names one request, so a result under an id that already has one replaces it (a request
  handled twice cannot push the other requests' outcomes out of the list). Update actions record nothing
  -- their feedback is `state.json`, as before. A failed result write is logged and swallowed: it never
  turns an applied request into a rejected one.
- **A routing request is "applied" once `routing.json` is written (#241).** `routing-set` writes the
  file, THEN calls `applyRouting` to re-register commands, then discovery is rewritten; a server that
  refuses its commands (Discord's 50001) is not a failed request and shows against that server in
  `discovery.json`, and an `applyRouting` that throws is logged and swallowed. `webhook-add` writes the
  secret first and the metadata second -- and if the second write fails, puts the previous secret back --
  so `routing.json` never names a webhook that has no URL, and the two files name different webhooks
  only if that rollback write also fails (which is logged, without the URL). `webhook-remove` writes in
  the reverse order and clears whichever of the two files holds
  the channel, so a webhook that was half removed can be removed again.
- **The panel's routing routes are server-native for the same reason `/api/plugins/request` is, plus one
  more: the SERVER mints the request id (#242).** The same reason: `requestedBy` comes from the verified
  session (`email:<email>` or `token`), never from the body. The extra one: `queueRoutingRequest` adds the
  `id` the page then looks up in `routing.results` (`HandlerConfig.newRequestId`, default
  `crypto.randomUUID()`), after the request's own fields, so a client cannot choose an id that would
  replace another request's recorded result (a result under an existing id replaces it, #241). Each body is
  REBUILT from validated fields (`parseRoutingSetInput` / `parseWebhookAddInput`; a `__proto__` server key
  is refused before it can be assigned), so an unknown key never reaches the mailbox. A webhook URL goes
  body -> parsed value -> the JSON payload on `bot-ops.sh`'s stdin and nowhere else: not `argv`, a log
  line, a response or an error message. A bad one (or one over 512 characters, which no real URL is) is a
  bare `400 bad webhook url`, a `runBotOps` that throws is caught (a fixed `502`; its message, redacted and
  clipped, is the log line), and whatever `bot-ops.sh` wrote (its stderr in a failure, its `queued` in a
  success) passes through `redactWebhookUrls` first: the
  panel's own copy of the bot's redactor with the decode step of the drain's detector in front (a JSON
  unicode escape, `\/` and a percent-escape are read, once, as the character they stand for), because the
  panel cannot import `src/`. Its snowflake, webhook-URL and plugin-name patterns are hand copies of the
  bot's, like the update-request ones, and a change to the bot's has to be made here too. The panel never
  calls Discord: there is no webhook preview route, the bot looks a webhook up when it applies a
  `webhook-add` and records the channel it landed on in `routing.results`. The four writes and
  `GET /api/routing` (`routing-get`, a plain `buildInvocation` branch) sit behind the same auth and
  cross-site checks as every `/api/` route; `server.test.ts` pins each of them per route rather than the
  routes carrying a second copy.
